\mathbb{G}_{i,j}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i},a_{j}):=1/\gamma\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}(Q_{i,j}^{B_{s^{\prime}}}(s,a_{i},a_{j})-Q_{i,j}^{\boldsymbol{0}}(s,a_{i},a_{j}))
, then by plugging in
V=B_{s^{\prime}}
and
V^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{0}
, we can derive
\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\mathbb{G}_{i,j}(\cdot\mid s,a_{i},a_{j})
for every
i
from
Equation

7
. Alternatively, we can take a functional derivative of
Equation

7

with respect to
V-V^{\prime}
, which means that there exist some functions
\{\mathbb{G}_{i,j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\mathbb{G}_{i,j}(\cdot\mid s,a_{i},a_{j})
for every
i
. Note that the decomposability of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}
above has to hold for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
.
Therefore, when
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, if
j\notin\mathcal{N}_{C}
, then there exists some
i\in\mathcal{N}
such that
(i,j)\notin\mathcal{E}_{Q}
. In this case,
\mathbb{P}
is not dependent on this
j
. So
\mathbb{P}
should be a function of the players in
\mathcal{N}_{C}
, which indicates that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{C},j\neq i}\mathbb{F}_{i,j}(\cdot\mid s,a_{i},a_{j})
for every
i
unless
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
. If
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
, then it directly concludes that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{o}(\cdot\mid s)
for some
\mathbb{F}_{o}
, since by the argument above, it should not depend on any player
j
. Next, we focus on the case when
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
,
and there are more than two players.

Specifically, in this case,
if there exist some
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
and
k_{1}\notin\mathcal{N}_{C}
, such that

\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C},i\neq k_{1}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{1},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{1}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C},i\neq k_{2}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{i}),

then we choose a fixed
a_{k_{1}}
but changing
a_{k_{2}}
arbitrarily. This preserves the equality, indicating that

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C},i\neq k_{1}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{1},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{1},\text{fix}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C},i\neq k_{2}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{i})

(8)

for any
a_{k_{2}}
. Since
k_{1}\notin\mathcal{N}_{C}
, we can have the left-hand side of (
8
) written as
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i})
by setting
\mathbb{F}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i}):=\mathbb{F}_{k_{1},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{1},\text{fix}},a_{i})
. Meanwhile, the right-hand side of (
8
) is also
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})
by definition, which concludes that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i})
, and proves the theorem.
In other words, as long as at least one
k_{1}
does not belong to
\mathcal{N}_{C}
, we can conclude the theorem.

If no such a
k_{1}\notin\mathcal{N}_{C}
exists, then it means that all players are in
\mathcal{N}_{C}
.
In this case, for any
k_{1}\neq k_{2}
, for a fixed
a_{k_{1},\text{fix}}
, we have

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{1},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{1},\text{fix}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{i})+\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},k_{1}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{k_{1},\text{fix}}).

(9)

Therefore, if we define
\mathbb{G}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i}):=\mathbb{F}_{k_{1},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{1},\text{fix}},a_{i})
for
i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1},k_{2}\}
, and
\mathbb{G}_{k_{2}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}}):=\mathbb{F}_{k_{1},k_{2}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{1},\text{fix}},a_{k_{2}})-\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},k_{1}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{k_{1},\text{fix}})
, then we have

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1},k_{2}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1}\}}\mathbb{G}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i}).

(10)

Let
k_{3}\in\mathcal{N}_{C}
such that
k_{3}\neq k_{1},k_{2}
. By definition of
\mathbb{F}_{i,j}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{3}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{3},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{2}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{i}).

Plugging (
10
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{3}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{3},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1}\}}\mathbb{G}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i})+\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},k_{1}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{k_{1}})

(11)

for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.
If we now fix
a_{k_{3}}
as
a_{k_{3},\text{fix}}
, then from (
11
) we know that

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{3}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{3},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3},\text{fix}},a_{i})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1},k_{3}\}}\mathbb{G}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i})+\mathbb{G}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3},\text{fix}})+\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},k_{1}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{k_{1}}).

(12)

Plugging (
12
) to (
11
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{1},k_{3}\}}\mathbb{G}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i})+\mathbb{F}_{k_{2},k_{1}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{2}},a_{k_{1}})+\mathbb{G}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3}})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{3}\}}\mathbb{F}_{k_{3},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3},\text{fix}},a_{i})-\mathbb{G}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3},\text{fix}})+\mathbb{G}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3}})=:\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i})

where
\mathbb{F}_{i}(\cdot|s,a_{i}):=\mathbb{F}_{k_{3},i}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3},\text{fix}},a_{i})
for
i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}/\{k_{3}\}
and
\mathbb{F}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3}}):=-\mathbb{G}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3},\text{fix}})+\mathbb{G}_{k_{3}}(\cdot|s,a_{k_{3}})
, which concludes the decomposability of the transition dynamics.
Finally, note that we can ensure the non-negativity of
\mathbb{F}_{i}
, since we can iterate the following procedure:

Algorithm 3

Procedure for constructing non-negative
\{\mathbb{F}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}

while
there exists
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}
such that
\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})<0

do

Set
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}
that satisfying
\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})<0

Sort
\mathcal{N}_{C}
according to the descending order of
b_{k}:=\min_{a_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{k}}\mathbb{F}_{k}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{k})
, and denote it as
\{j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{|\mathcal{N}_{C}|}\}

Define
\text{tmp1}=0,t=1

while

\text{tmp1}<-\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})

do

Define
\text{tmp2}=-\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})-\text{tmp1}>0

Define
\text{tmp3}=\min(\min_{a_{j_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{t}}}\mathbb{F}_{j_{t}}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j_{t}}),\text{tmp2})>0
;

Update
\mathbb{F}_{j_{t}}(s^{\prime}|s,a_{j_{t}})\leftarrow\mathbb{F}_{j_{t}}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j_{t}})-\text{tmp3}
for all
a_{j_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{t}}

Update
\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})\leftarrow\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})+\text{tmp3}
for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}

Update
\text{tmp1}\leftarrow\text{tmp1}+\text{tmp3}
and
t\leftarrow t+1

end

while

end

while

In this context, the third and fourth lines of the inner-while loop ensure that
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}|s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{j}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j})
always holds for
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
. Furthermore,
\text{tmp}3
remains greater than 0 within the inner-while loop. This is because if
\min_{a_{j_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{t}}}\mathbb{F}_{j_{t}}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j_{t}})\leq 0
, then it implies that
\min_{a_{j_{k}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{k}}}\mathbb{F}_{j_{k}}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j_{k}})=0
for all
k\in[t]
. Also,
\min_{a_{j_{k}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{k}}}\mathbb{F}_{j_{k}}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j_{k}})\leq\min_{a_{j_{t}}\in\mathcal{A}_{j_{t}}}\mathbb{F}_{j_{t}}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j_{t}})\leq 0
for every
k\in[|\mathcal{N}_{C}|]
. Meanwhile, since we assumed
\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})<0
, we can define a vector
\widetilde{a}
with elements
\widetilde{a}_{j}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{a_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}}\mathbb{F}_{j}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j})
. This implies that
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\widetilde{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})<0
, which contradicts the condition
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\widetilde{a})\geq 0
.

Therefore, our procedure ensures that the number of pairs
(s,s^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{S}}\times{\mathcal{S}}
and indexes
i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}
for which
\min_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})<0
is consistently reduced.

If there are only two players, then both of them belong to
\mathcal{N}_{C}
. However, one cannot decompose the transition dynamics as above, as there is no such a
k_{3}\neq k_{1},k_{2}
to construct the aforementioned formula. Indeed, any two-player (zero-sum) MG satisfies our definition of zero-sum NMGs in
Definition

1
.

For the reward decomposition, if
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, we have

\displaystyle r_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\left(Q_{i,j}^{V}(s,a_{i},a_{j})-\gamma\langle\boldsymbol{1}(j\in\mathcal{N}_{C})\mathbb{F}_{j}(\cdot\mid s,a_{j}),V(\cdot)\rangle\right)

and if
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
, we have

\displaystyle r_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\left(Q_{i,j}^{V}(s,a_{i},a_{j})-\gamma\langle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}|}\mathbb{F}_{o}(\cdot\mid s),V(\cdot)\rangle\right).

Hence,
r_{i}(s,a_{i},\cdot)
can be represented as
r_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for some functions
(r_{i,j})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
. The same procedure as
Algorithm

3
provides that we can ensure the non-negativity of
r_{i,j}
, so that
r_{i}(s,a_{i},\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to
\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}
. In fact, adding any large-enough constant to
r_{i,j}
does not change the solution to the problem, while ensuring the non-negativity of
r_{i,j}
.
∎

Proposition 1 - Finite-horizon version.
For a given graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
, an MG
(\mathcal{N},{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{A},(\mathbb{P}_{h})_{h\in[H]},(r_{h,i})_{i\in\mathcal{N},h\in[H]})
with more than two players is an NMG with
\mathcal{G}
if and only if: (1)
r_{h,i}(s,a_{i},\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to

\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}
for each
i\in\mathcal{N}\,,s\in{\mathcal{S}}\,,a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i},\,h\in[H]
, i.e.,
r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{h,i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for a set of functions
\{r_{h,i,j}(s,a_{i},\cdot)\}_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}
and (2)
the transition dynamics
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to
\mathcal{N}_{C}
corresponding to this
\mathcal{G}
for all
h\in[H]
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{h,i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})
for a set of functions
\{\mathbb{F}_{h,i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\cdot)\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}
if
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, or
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{h,o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
for some constant function (of
\bm{a}
)
\mathbb{F}_{h,o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
if
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
.
Moreover, an MG qualifies as a zero-sum NMG if and only if it satisfies an additional condition: the NG, characterized by
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{A},(r_{h,i,j}(s))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}})
, must be a zero-sum NG for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}},h\in[H]
. In the case of two players, every (zero-sum) Markov game becomes a (zero-sum) NMG.

Proposition 2
(Decomposition of
(Q_{i}^{V})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
)
.

For an infinite-horizon
\gamma
-discounted NMG with
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
such that
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, if we know that
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})
, and
r_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for some
\{\mathbb{F}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}
and
\{r_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
, then the
Q_{i,j}^{V}
given in
Definition

1
can be represented as

\displaystyle Q_{i,j}^{V}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})+\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\gamma\left(\boldsymbol{1}(j\in\mathcal{N}_{C})\mathbb{F}_{j}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j})+\boldsymbol{1}(i\in\mathcal{N}_{C})\lambda_{i,j}(s)\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})\right)V(s^{\prime})

for any non-negative
(\lambda_{i,j}(s))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\lambda_{i,j}(s)=1
for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
For an infinite-horizon
\gamma
-discounted NMG with
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
such that
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
, if we know that
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
, and
r_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for some
\mathbb{F}_{o}
and
\{r_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
, then
the
Q_{i,j}^{V}
given in
Definition

1
can be represented as

\displaystyle Q_{i,j}^{V}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})+\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\gamma\left(\lambda_{i,j}(s)\mathbb{F}_{o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\right)V(s^{\prime})

for any non-negative
(\lambda_{i,j}(s))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\lambda_{i,j}(s)=1
for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
. We call it the
canonical
decomposition of
\{Q_{i}^{V}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
when
Q_{i,j}^{V}
can be represented as above with
\lambda_{i,j}(s)={1}/{|\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}|}
for
j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}
.

Proposition

2
naturally follows from the proof of
Proposition

1
(a).

Proposition 2 - Finite-horizon version
(Decomposition of
(Q_{h,i}^{V})_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
)
.

For a finite-horizon NMG with
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
with
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, we know
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{h,i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})
, and
r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{h,i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for some
\{\mathbb{F}_{h,i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}
and
\{r_{h,i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
, then the
Q_{h,i,j}^{V}
given in
Definition

1
can be represented as

\displaystyle Q_{h,i,j}^{V}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=r_{h,i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})+\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\boldsymbol{1}(j\in\mathcal{N}_{C})\mathbb{F}_{h,j}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{j})+\boldsymbol{1}(i\in\mathcal{N}_{C})\lambda_{h,i,j}(s)\mathbb{F}_{h,i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})\right)V_{h+1}(s^{\prime})

for any non-negative
(\lambda_{h,i,j}(s))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\lambda_{h,i,j}(s)=1
for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
,
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, and
h\in[H]
. For a finite-horizon NMG with
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
such that
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
,
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{h,o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
, and
r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{h,i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for some
\mathbb{F}_{h,o}
and
\{r_{h,i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
,
the
Q_{h,i,j}^{V}
given in
Definition

1
can be represented as

\displaystyle Q_{h,i,j}^{V}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=r_{h,i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})+\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\lambda_{h,i,j}(s)\mathbb{F}_{h,o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)\right)V_{h+1}(s^{\prime})

for any non-negative
(\lambda_{h,i,j}(s))_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
such that
\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\lambda_{h,i,j}(s)=1
for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
,
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, and
h\in[H]
. We call it the
canonical
decomposition of
Q
-value functions when
Q_{h,i,j}^{V}
can be represented as above with
\lambda_{h,i,j}(s)={1}/{|\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}|}
for
j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}
.

B.2
An alternative definition of NMGs

Definition 3

(An alternative definition of NMGs)
.

An infinite-horizon
\gamma
-discounted MG is called a
Multi-player MG with Networked separable interactions (NMG)
characterized by a tuple

(\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q}),{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P},(r_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},\gamma)

if for any
policy

\pi
, there exist a set of functions
(Q_{i,j}^{\pi})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
and an undirected connected graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
such that
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}Q_{i,j}^{\pi}(s,a_{i},a_{j})

holds for every
i\in\mathcal{N}
,
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
. A finite-horizon MG is called a
Multi-player MG with Networked separable interactions
if for any
policy

\pi
, there exist a set of functions
(Q_{h,i,j}^{\pi})_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q},h\in[H]}
such that
Q_{h,i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}Q_{h,i,j}^{\pi}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
holds for every
i\in\mathcal{N}
,
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
, and
h\in[H]
.

The “if” condition of
Proposition

1
also holds for this definition. However, the proof for the “only if” condition of
Proposition

1
uses the functional derivative argument. We cannot use the functional derivative here directly, since
\bm{V}^{\pi}:=(V^{\pi}(s))_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}
cannot represent all vectors in
[0,R/(1-\gamma)]^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
. To be specific, we have

\displaystyle Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}Q_{i,j}^{\pi}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,\bm{a}),V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle

for any policies
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
,
which indicates

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}(Q_{i,j}^{\pi}(s,a_{i},a_{j})-Q_{i,j}^{\pi^{\prime}}(s,a_{i},a_{j}))=\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid s,\bm{a}),V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)-V_{i}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\cdot)\rangle.

(13)

If we can find a set of policies
(\pi^{(k)})_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
such that the vectors in
\{\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi^{(k)}}-\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi^{\prime}}\}_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
are independent for some fixed
\pi^{\prime}
, then we can concatenate the vectors
\{\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi^{(k)}}-\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi^{\prime}}\}_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
together as a matrix of size
|{\mathcal{S}}|\times|{\mathcal{S}}|
that is full-rank, and solve for
\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})
by solving the linear equations (
13
), for some fixed
(s,\bm{a})
. This way, we can show that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\mathbb{F}_{i,j}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,a_{i},a_{j})
, and the rest of the proof follows from that of
Proposition

1
. However, such a set of policies
(\pi^{(k)})_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
(and
\pi^{\prime}
) may not exist in some degenerate cases, as to be detailed below.

Definition 4

(Degenerate MG with respect to player
i
)
.

We call an MG
degenerate
with respect to player
i\in\mathcal{N}
if there exists some
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that for any
\pi
,
Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a})
is a constant function of
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 5

(Non-degenerate MG)
.

We call an MG
non-degenerate
if an MG is not degenerate with respect to any player
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

Now, we are ready to state the counterpart of
Proposition

1
in these non-degenerate cases. The proof for the “if” direction is exactly the same as that of
Proposition

1
. We focus on the proof of the “only if” statement.

Proposition 1 - An alternative definition version.
For a given graph
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}_{Q})
, a
non-degenerate
MG
(\mathcal{N},{\mathcal{S}},\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P},(r_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}},\gamma)
(in the sense of
Definition

5
) with more than two players is an NMG with
\mathcal{G}
if and only if:
(1)
r_{i}(s,a_{i},\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to

\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}
for each
i\in\mathcal{N},s\in{\mathcal{S}},a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, i.e.,
r_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
for a set of functions
\{r_{i,j}(s,a_{i},\cdot)\}_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}
, and (2)
the transition dynamics
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to
the
\mathcal{N}_{C}
corresponding to this
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.,
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})
for a set of functions
\{\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\cdot)\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}
if
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, or
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
for some constant function (of
\bm{a}
)
\mathbb{F}_{o}(s^{\prime}\mid s)
if
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Proofs of the claims are deferred to
Section

B.3
to preserve the flow of the argument.

Claim 1
.

For player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, if there exists a policy
\pi
such that for every
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, there exist
\bm{a}_{s,1},\bm{a}_{s,2}
that make
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{s,1})\neq Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{s,2})
, then we can construct
|{\mathcal{S}}|
number of policies
(\pi^{(k)})_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
such that
\{\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi^{(k)}}-\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi}\}_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
are independent for any fixed
\pi
.

Therefore, for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, if the condition of
1
holds, then we can guarantee that there exist some functions
\{\mathbb{G}_{i,j}\}_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\mathbb{G}_{i,j}(\cdot|s,a_{i},a_{j})
, by the argument after
Equation

13
. If we assume that the condition of
1
holds for every player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, then there exist some functions
\{\mathbb{G}_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{E}_{Q}}
such that
\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\mathbb{G}_{i,j}(\cdot|s,a_{i},a_{j})
for every
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Then, we can prove the decomposability of
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\cdot)
with respect to
\mathcal{N}_{C}
with the same steps as Equations (
8
) - (
12
).

Hence, by
1
, we only need to prove that if an MG is non-degenerate with respect to player
i
, then there exists a policy
\pi
such that there exist
\bm{a}_{s,1},\bm{a}_{s,2}
that make
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{s,1})\neq Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{s,2})
for every
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
.
If
|\mathcal{A}|=1
, then the transition dynamics are already decomposed, so we do not need to consider this case.

Claim 2
.

Assume
|\mathcal{A}|\geq 2
. For player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, assume that for any policy
\pi
, there exists a state
s_{\pi}\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
Q^{\pi}_{i}(s_{\pi},\bm{a})
is a constant function of
\bm{a}
. Then, there exists a state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that uniformly for any policy
\pi
,
Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a})
is a constant function of
\bm{a}
.

2
shows that if the assumption of
1
does not hold for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, then

{\mathcal{S}}_{\text{const},i}:=\{s\mid\text{For any }\pi,Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a})\text{ is a constant function of }\bm{a}\}

is not an empty set. By
Definition

4
,
if an MG is not degenerate with respect to player
i
, then
{\mathcal{S}}_{\text{const},i}
is empty, and thus the conditions of
1
always hold for player
i
. Therefore, if we assume the non-degeneracy of MG (
Definition

5
), by
1
and the arguments immediately following it,
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to
\mathcal{N}_{C}
.
∎

Remark 6

(Degenerate MG with respect to player
i
)
.

The degeneracy of MGs as defined above
can indeed be rare.
To illustrate, consider a seemingly degenerate scenario where for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
r_{i}(s,\bm{a})
remains a constant function with respect to
\bm{a}
; even under such circumstances, it is possible for the MG to be non-degenerate with respect to player
i
. For example, assume that
\mathcal{N}=[2],\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
,
{\mathcal{S}}=\{-1,1\}

\mathcal{A}_{i}=\{-1,1\}
,
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,a_{1},a_{2})=\boldsymbol{1}(s^{\prime}=sa_{1}a_{2}),r_{1}(s,\bm{a})=r_{2}(s,\bm{a})=(1+s)
. Let
\pi
satisfy
\pi(\bm{a}{\,|\,}1)=\boldsymbol{1}(a_{1}=1,a_{2}=-1),\pi(\bm{a}{\,|\,}-1)=\boldsymbol{1}(a_{1}=-1,a_{2}=-1)
. Then, we have
V^{\pi}_{1}(1)=2+\frac{1}{2}V^{\pi}_{1}(-1)
and
V^{\pi}_{1}(-1)=\frac{1}{2}V^{\pi}_{1}(-1)
, which further means
V^{\pi}_{1}(-1)=0
and
V^{\pi}_{1}(1)=2
, i.e.,
V^{\pi}_{1}(s)=1+s
always holds. As a result,
Q^{\pi}_{1}(1,\bm{a})=1+s+\frac{1}{2}\left(sa_{1}a_{2}+1\right)
,
which is not a constant function, and the game is thus non-degenerate with respect to agent
i
.
Moreover, suppose that the policy, transition dynamics, and reward functions are randomly chosen. The measure of the event that the existence of
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a})
is constant for all possible actions
\bm{a}
under this randomly chosen policy, transition dynamics, and reward function is 0. This is primarily because
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\cdot):\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
must lie on a particular hyperplane in the overall value function space, which takes measure
0
. Oftentimes, different actions will transition to different states and yield different rewards, thereby generating almost unique
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a})
values.

Remark 7
.

If we have more than two players that satisfy the condition of
1
, i.e., the MG is non-degenerate with respect to more than two players (instead of being non-degenerate with respect to all players), then we can still guarantee the decomposability of
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\cdot)
with respect to a set that is not necessarily the same as
\mathcal{N}_{C}
. As a byproduct of the decomposability of
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\cdot)
, we can guarantee the decomposability of
r_{i}(s,a_{i},\cdot)
, too.

B.3
Deferred proof of the claims in
Section

B.2

We define
\Pi:=\begin{bmatrix}\pi(s_{1})&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\pi(s_{2})&\cdots&0\\
\vbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}}&\vbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}}&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{$.$}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 1.0mu}&0\\
0&0&\cdots&\pi(s_{|{\mathcal{S}}|})\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}||\mathcal{A}|\times|{\mathcal{S}}|}
, where
\pi(s)
is a column vector for the policy at state
s
,
\bm{P}:=(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}))_{((s,\bm{a}),s^{\prime})}
,
\bm{R}:=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{r}(s_{1})^{\intercal}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\bm{r}(s_{2})^{\intercal}&\cdots&0\\
\vbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}}&\vbox{\kern 6.0pt\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}\hbox{$.$}}&\mathinner{\mkern 1.0mu\raise 7.0pt\vbox{\kern 7.0pt\hbox{$.$}}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 4.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 2.0mu\raise 1.0pt\hbox{$.$}\mkern 1.0mu}&0\\
0&0&\cdots&\bm{r}(s_{|{\mathcal{S}}|})^{\intercal}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times|{\mathcal{S}}||\mathcal{A}|}
, then we have
\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi}=(I-\gamma\Pi^{\intercal}\bm{P})^{-1}\bm{R}\Pi\boldsymbol{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
where
\boldsymbol{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
is
(1,\dots,1)^{\intercal}
.

Proof of
1
.

If we differentiate
\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi}
with respect to
\pi
along the direction
\Delta_{(s,\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2})}:=e_{\bm{a}_{1},s}-e_{\bm{a}_{2},s}{\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}||{\mathcal{S}}|}}
for some
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
, i.e., the direction that increases (or decreases)
\pi(\bm{a}_{1}{\,|\,}s)
and decreases (or increases)
\pi(\bm{a}_{2}{\,|\,}s)
, respectively, then by computation, we can have the following directional derivative:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Delta_{(s,\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2})}}\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi}

\displaystyle=(I-\gamma\Pi^{\intercal}\bm{P})^{-1}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a}_{1})V_{i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})-\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}_{2})V_{i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})\right)+r_{i}(s,\bm{a}_{1})-r_{i}(s,\bm{a}_{2})\right)e_{s}

\displaystyle=\left(Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{1})-Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{2})\right)(I-\gamma\Pi^{\intercal}\bm{P})^{-1}e_{s}.

(14)

Therefore, if there exists a policy
\pi
such that for every
s
, there exist
\bm{a}_{s,1},\bm{a}_{s,2}
that make
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{s,1})\neq Q_{i}^{\pi}(s,\bm{a}_{s,2})
, then deviating from
\pi
along the
\Delta_{(s,\bm{a}_{s,1},\bm{a}_{s,2})}
direction for every
s
provides
\{\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi^{(k)}}-\bm{V}_{i}^{\pi}\}_{k\in[|{\mathcal{S}}|]}
vectors that are independent of each other, since
(I-\gamma\Pi^{\intercal}\bm{P})^{-1}
is an invertible matrix for any
\Pi
.
∎

Proof of
2
.

Note that
\pi\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
. For all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, define
\Pi_{s}
as

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}:=\{\pi{\,|\,}\text{$Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a})$ is a constant function of $\bm{a}$ }\},

where we omit the dependence on
i
as we focus on the discussion on a specific
i
here.
For any
\pi
, there exists a state
s_{\pi}
such that
Q_{i}^{\pi}(s_{\pi},\bm{a})
is a constant function of
\bm{a}
, which means that
\pi\in\Pi_{s_{\pi}}
and thus
\Pi_{s_{\pi}}\neq\emptyset
, which further yields
\sumop\displaylimits_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left(\text{measure of }(\Pi_{s})\right)\geq\left(\text{measure of the whole space of }(\Delta(\mathcal{A})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|})\right)>0
. By the pigeonhole principle, we know that there exists some
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
such that
\left(\text{measure of }(\Pi_{s})\right)>0
since
{\mathcal{S}}
is a finite set.

Note that for the above
s
such that
\left(\text{measure of }(\Pi_{s})\right)>0
, for any pair of
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
,
Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a}_{1})-Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a}_{2})
can be represented by the ratio of polynomials of
\pi
while it has a non-zero measure set of solution, we can conclude that
Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a}_{1})-Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a}_{2})=0
for every
\pi\in\Delta(\mathcal{A})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
,
\bm{a}_{1},\bm{a}_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
for
s
such that
\left(\text{measure of }(\Pi_{s})\right)>0
. Therefore, for the
s
such that
\left(\text{measure of }(\Pi_{s})\right)>0
, we have that for every
\pi
,
Q^{\pi}_{i}(s,\bm{a})
is a constant function of
\bm{a}
.
∎

B.4
Counterexample for Alternative
Definition

3

We now show via a counterexample that the alternative definition given in
Definition

3
may not even preserve the networked separable structure of the reward functions in general.

Consider a Markov game with the following specifications:
\mathcal{N}=[3]
,
\gamma=\frac{1}{2}
,
{\mathcal{S}}=\{s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\}
,
\mathcal{A}_{i}=\{0,1\}
,
\mathbb{P}(s_{3}{\,|\,}s_{3},\bm{a})=\mathbb{P}(s_{2}{\,|\,}s_{2},\bm{a})=1
for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathbb{P}(s_{2}{\,|\,}s_{1},\bm{a})=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1)^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}
,
\mathbb{P}(s_{3}{\,|\,}s_{1},\bm{a})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1)^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}
,
r_{1}(s_{3},\bm{a})=1
and
r_{1}(s_{2},\bm{a})=2
for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
, and
r_{1}(s_{1},\bm{a})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1)^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}}
.
Then, we have by definition that for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,
Q_{1}^{\pi}(s_{2},\bm{a})=V_{1}^{\pi}(s_{2})=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}r_{1}(s_{2},\bm{a})=4
,
Q_{1}^{\pi}(s_{3},\bm{a})=V_{1}^{\pi}(s_{3})=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}r_{1}(s_{3},\bm{a})=2
, and

\displaystyle Q_{1}^{\pi}(s_{1},\bm{a})

\displaystyle=r_{1}(s_{1},\bm{a})+\gamma\mathbb{P}(s_{3}{\,|\,}s_{1},\bm{a})V^{\pi}_{1}(s_{3})+\gamma\mathbb{P}(s_{2}{\,|\,}s_{1},\bm{a})V^{\pi}_{1}(s_{2})

\displaystyle=r_{1}(s_{1},\bm{a})+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1)^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}})\cdot 2+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot(-1)^{a_{1}a_{2}a_{3}})\cdot 4=2.

Hence,
Q^{\pi}_{1}(s,a_{1},\cdot)
is decomposable with respect to {2, 3}, however, the reward
r_{1}(s,a_{1},\cdot)
is not, due to
r_{1}(s_{1},\bm{a})
. Note that this counterexample exactly exhibits the importance of the ergodicity of the Markov chain in removing the degeneracy.

B.5
Reviewing existing results for zero-sum NGs

In a zero-sum NG, i.e., a zero-sum polymatrix game, for each player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, the reward
r_{i}\left(a_{i},\pi_{-i}\right)
of using a pure strategy
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
is a linear function of
\pi_{-i}
. The following linear program, which involves variables
\pi\in\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
and
\bm{v}=\left(v_{i}\right)_{i\in\mathcal{N}}
, aims to minimize the sum of the variables
v_{i}
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\min_{\bm{v},\pi}&\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}v_{i}\\
\text{subject to }&v_{i}\geq r_{i}\left(e_{a_{i}},\pi_{-i}\right),\quad\text{ for all }i\in\mathcal{N},a\in\mathcal{A}_{i},\\
&\pi\in\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}).\end{array}

Reference
[
24
]
states that if
(\pi^{\star},\bm{v}^{\star})
is an optimal solution to the above linear program, then
\pi^{\star}
is an NE of the zero-sum NG, and the optimal value of the above linear program is 0. Conversely if
\pi^{\star}
is an NE, then there exists a
\bm{v}^{\star}
such that
(\pi^{\star},\bm{v}^{\star})
is an optimal solution to the above linear program and
\bm{v}^{\star}
is the expected reward vector under
\pi^{\star}
. By observation, we additionally have the following proposition as an extension:

Proposition 5
.

If
(\pi^{\star},\bm{v}^{\star})
is an
\epsilon
-optimal solution to the above linear program, then
\pi^{\star}
is an
\epsilon
-NE and
r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\leq v_{i}^{\star}\leq r_{i}(\pi^{\star})+\epsilon
for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Conversely, if
\pi^{\star}
is an
\epsilon
-NE, then there exists a
\bm{v}^{\star}
such that
(\pi^{\star},\bm{v}^{\star})
is an
n\epsilon
-optimal solution to the above linear program.

Proof.

If
(\pi^{\star},\bm{v}^{\star})
is an
\epsilon
-optimal solution to the above linear program (whose optimal solution is exactly
0
), we have

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}v_{i}^{\star}\underset{(i)}{=}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(v_{i}^{\star}-r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\right)\underset{(ii)}{\geq}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}r_{i}\left(\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}^{\star}\right)-r_{i}\left(\pi^{\star}\right)\right)

which proves that
\pi^{\star}
is an
\epsilon
-NE: here
(i)
holds since the sum of reward over players is zero, and
(ii)
holds due to the constraint of the given linear program. Moreover, we can also observe that
r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\leq v_{i}^{\star}\leq r_{i}(\pi^{\star})+\epsilon
holds, since
0\leq\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}r_{i}\left(\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}^{\star}\right)-r_{i}\left(\pi^{\star}\right)\leq v_{i}^{\star}-r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\leq\epsilon
for each
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

Conversely, suppose that
\pi^{\star}
is an
\epsilon
-NE. Then, defining
v^{\star}_{i}:=\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}r_{i}\left(\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}^{\star}\right)
satisfies the constraints of the given linear program. In addition, we have

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}v_{i}^{\star}=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(v_{i}^{\star}-r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}r_{i}\left(\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}^{\star}\right)-r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\right)\leq n\epsilon,

which concludes the theorem.
∎

Proposition 6
.

Suppose
\pi^{\star}
is an
\epsilon
-approximate CCE of the zero-sum NG. Then the product of its marginalized policy
\widehat{\pi}^{\star}
is an
n\epsilon
-approximate NE of the zero-sum NG.
Moreover, it holds that
r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\geq r_{i}(\widehat{\pi}^{\star})\geq r_{i}(\pi^{\star})-n\epsilon
for every
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

A similar method with
[
24
]
’s Theorem 2 can provide proof of
Proposition

6
. To be specific, define
v^{\star}_{i}:=r_{i}(\pi^{\star})
, then we have
(\widehat{\pi}^{\star},\bm{v}^{\star})
is an
n\epsilon
-optimal solution. By
Proposition

5
, we can conclude that
\widehat{\pi}^{\star}
is an
n\epsilon
-approximate NE and
r_{i}(\pi^{\star})\geq r_{i}(\widehat{\pi}^{\star})\geq r_{i}(\pi^{\star})-n\epsilon
for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
.
∎

We note that
Proposition

5
and
Proposition

6
are not in
[
24
]
, but it plays an important role in proving
Proposition

3
.

B.6
Omitted proof of
Proposition

3

Before introducing the proof of
Proposition

3
, we provide the relationship between approximate Markov stationary CCE and approximate auxiliary-game CCE in Markov games.

Claim 3
.

For an infinite-horizon
\gamma
-discounted MG, an
\epsilon
-approximate Markov stationary CCE
\pi
of this MG makes
\pi(s)

an
\epsilon
-approximate CCE of the auxiliary game at each state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, where the auxiliary game payoff matrix at each
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
is defined as
(r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a}),V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle)_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\epsilon
-approximate Markov (perfect) CCE, we have for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and all
i\in\mathcal{N}
that

\displaystyle V_{i}^{\pi}(s)\leq\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}}}V_{i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(s)\leq V_{i}^{\pi}(s)+\epsilon.

(15)

Then, by one-step of Bellman equation for
\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}}}V_{i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(s)
, we also know that

\displaystyle\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}}}V_{i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(s)=\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{-i}(s)}\bigg[r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}}}V_{i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg].

(16)

Moreover, we have by the left inequality of
Equation

15
that

\displaystyle\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{-i}(s)}\bigg[r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}}}V_{i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{-i}(s)}\bigg[r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg].

(17)

Also, we have by one-step Bellman consistency equation for
V_{i}^{\pi}(s)
that

\displaystyle V_{i}^{\pi}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi(s)}\bigg[r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg].

(18)

Combining
Equations

15
,
17
and
18
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{-i}(s)}\bigg[r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi(s)}\bigg[r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg]+\epsilon,

for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
, which proves that
\pi(s)
is an
\epsilon
-approximate CCE of the auxiliary game, where the game payoff matrix at each state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
is
(r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot|s,\bm{a}),V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle)_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
.
∎

Claim 4
.

For an
H
-horizon MG and
h\in[H]
, an
\epsilon
-approximate Markov CCE
\pi=\{\pi_{h}\}_{h\in[H]}
of this MG makes
\pi_{h}(s)
an
\epsilon
-approximate CCE of the auxiliary game at each state
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and each
h\in[H]
, where the auxiliary game payoff matrix at
(s,h)
is defined as
(r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})+\langle\mathbb{P}_{h}(\cdot|s,\bm{a}),V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle)_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
, where
V_{H+1,i}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(s)=0
for any policy
\widetilde{\pi}
, and for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
i\in\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

By definition of
\epsilon
-approximate Markov (perfect) CCE, we have that for all
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
h\in[H]
, and
i\in\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle V_{h,i}^{\pi}(s)\leq\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times H}}}V_{h,i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(s)\leq V_{h,i}^{\pi}(s)+\epsilon.

(19)

Then, by one-step of Bellman equation for
\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times H}}}V_{h,i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(s)
, we also know that

\displaystyle\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times H}}}V_{h,i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(s)=\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{{h,-i}}(s)}\bigg[r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})+\langle\mathbb{P}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times H}}}V_{h+1,i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg].

(20)

Moreover, we have by the left inequality of
Equation

19
and
V_{H+1,i}^{\widetilde{\pi}}(s)=0
for any
\widetilde{\pi}
that

\displaystyle\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{{h,-i}}(s)}\bigg[r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})+\langle\mathbb{P}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i}){{}^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times(H-1)}}}V_{h+1,i}^{\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg]

(21)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\max_{\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\mathbb{E}_{\nu,\pi_{{h,-i}}(s)}\bigg[r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})+\langle\mathbb{P}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg].

Also, we have by one-step Bellman equation for
V_{h,i}^{\pi}(s)
that

\displaystyle V_{h,i}^{\pi}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi_{h}(s)}\bigg[r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})+\langle\mathbb{P}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),~V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle\bigg].

(22)

Combining
Equations

19
,
20
and
22
, we can conclude the theorem.
∎

See
3

Proof.

For an arbitrary joint Markov policy
\mu
, we define

d_{s^{\prime},\mu}(s):=(1-\gamma)\mathbb{E}_{s_{1}=s^{\prime},\mu}[\sumop\displaylimits_{t=1}^{\infty}\gamma^{t-1}\boldsymbol{1}(s_{t}=s)]
which is the discounted visitation measure of states
when we follow policy
\mu
and start from state
s^{\prime}
. Here,
s_{t}
denotes the state at timestep
t
.
We use
\pi
to denote an
\epsilon
-approximate Markov CCE, and
\widehat{\pi}
to denote the product policy of the per-state marginalized policies of
\pi
for all agents
i\in\mathcal{N}
. We define
\pi_{i}
as the per-state marginalized policy for player
i
from
\pi
. With this notation, we have
\widehat{\pi}:=\pi_{1}\times\cdots\times\pi_{n}
. Note that for zero-sum NMGs, by
Proposition

1
we know that if
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})
or
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}\mid s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{h,i}(s^{\prime}\mid s,a_{i})
, for infinite- and finite-horizon cases, respectively; and if
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset
, we have
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{o}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s)
or
\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})=\mathbb{F}_{h,o}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s)
for the two cases.

In the infinite-horizon case, by
3
, we know that
\pi(s)
also serves as an
\epsilon
-approximate CCE for an auxiliary game with a payoff matrix
(r_{i}(s,\bm{a})+\gamma\langle\mathbb{P}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),V_{i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle)_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Since this auxiliary game is a zero-sum NG by the definition of zero-sum NMG,
Proposition

6
implies that the policy
\widehat{\pi}{(s)}
is an
n\epsilon
-approximate NE of the auxiliary game with the same payoff matrix, and the following inequality is valid for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
:

\displaystyle V_{i}^{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle=r_{i}(s,\pi)+\gamma\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})\pi(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)V_{i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq r_{i}(s,\widehat{\pi})+\gamma\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})\widehat{\pi}(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)V_{i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})+n\epsilon.

(23)

Applying the inequality
V_{i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})\leq r_{i}(s^{\prime},\widehat{\pi})+\gamma\sumop\displaylimits_{\widetilde{s}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}(\widetilde{s}{\,|\,}s^{\prime},\bm{a})\widehat{\pi}(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})V_{i}^{\pi}(\widetilde{s})+n\epsilon
into the final expression of
Equation

23
, and applying it recursively, we have that for every
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
:

\displaystyle V_{i}^{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle\leq V_{i}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)+n\epsilon/(1-\gamma).

(24)

Moreover, we have that for any
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}

\displaystyle V^{\widehat{\pi}}_{i}(s)\geq V^{\pi}_{i}(s)-n\epsilon/(1-\gamma)

\displaystyle\geq V^{\mu,\pi_{-i}}_{i}(s)-(n+1)\epsilon/(1-\gamma)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{a}\sim{\mu(s^{\prime})\times\pi_{-i}(s^{\prime})},s^{\prime}\sim d_{s,\mu,\pi_{-i}}}[r_{i}(s^{\prime},\bm{a})]-(n+1)\epsilon{/(1-\gamma)}

\displaystyle\underset{(i)}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{a}\sim{\mu(s^{\prime})\times\widehat{\pi}_{-i}(s^{\prime})},s^{\prime}\sim d_{s,\mu,{\pi}_{-i}}}[r_{i}(s^{\prime},\bm{a})]-(n+1)\epsilon/(1-\gamma)

\displaystyle\underset{(ii)}{=}\mathbb{E}_{\bm{a}\sim{\mu(s^{\prime})\times\widehat{\pi}_{-i}(s^{\prime})},s^{\prime}\sim d_{s,\mu,\widehat{\pi}_{-i}}}[r_{i}(s^{\prime},\bm{a})]-(n+1)\epsilon/(1-\gamma)

\displaystyle=V^{\mu,\widehat{\pi}_{-i}}_{i}(s)-(n+1)\epsilon/(1-\gamma),

where the second inequality follows from
\pi
being an
\epsilon
-approximate Markov CCE, and
(i)
holds since for arbitrary
(\nu_{i},\nu_{-i})\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}\times\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{-i})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|}
and for any
\nu_{\mathcal{S}}\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{a}\sim\nu_{i}(s^{\prime})\times\nu_{-i}(s^{\prime}),s^{\prime}\sim\nu_{\mathcal{S}}}[r_{i}(s^{\prime},\bm{a})]

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\nu_{\mathcal{S}}(s^{\prime})r_{i}(s^{\prime},\bm{a})\nu_{i}(a_{i}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})\nu_{-i}(\bm{a}_{-i}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\nu_{\mathcal{S}}(s^{\prime})r_{i,j}(s^{\prime},a_{i},a_{j})\nu_{i}(a_{i}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})\nu_{-i}(\bm{a}_{-i}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{Q,i}}{\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\nu_{\mathcal{S}}(s^{\prime})r_{i,j}(s^{\prime},a_{i},a_{j})\nu_{i}(a_{i}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})\nu_{j}(a_{j}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\bm{a}\sim\nu_{i}(s^{\prime})\times\widehat{\nu}_{-i}(s^{\prime}),s^{\prime}\sim\nu_{\mathcal{S}}}[r_{i}(s^{\prime},\bm{a})],

where
\widehat{\nu}:=\nu_{1}\times\dots\times\nu_{n}
is the product policy of the per-state marginalized policies of
\nu
, and
(ii)
holds due to the following fact: if
\mathcal{N}_{C}\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s)

\displaystyle:=\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})\pi(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)=\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,a_{i})\pi(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,a_{i})\pi(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}_{C}}\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\mathbb{F}_{i}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,a_{i})\pi_{i}(a_{i}{\,|\,}s)=:\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s),

(25)

or if
\mathcal{N}_{C}=\emptyset

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\pi}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s)=\mathbb{F}_{o}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s)=\mathbb{P}_{\widehat{\pi}}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s).

(26)

In other words, the marginalized policy’s state visitation measure
d_{s,{\widehat{\pi}}}
is the same as the original policy’s state visitation measure
d_{s,{\pi}}
. Therefore, marginalizing
\epsilon
-approximate Markov CCE provides
(n+1)\epsilon/(1-\gamma)
-approximate Markov NE.

Moreover, a similar argument holds for the finite-horizon episodic setting.
In the
H
-horizon case, since
\pi
is an
\epsilon
-approximate Markov CCE, by
4

\pi_{h}(s)
also serves as an
\epsilon
-approximate CCE for an auxiliary game with a payoff matrix defined by
(r_{h,i}(s,\bm{a})+\langle\mathbb{P}_{h}(\cdot{\,|\,}s,\bm{a}),V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(\cdot)\rangle)_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}
for player
i\in\mathcal{N}
. Since this auxiliary game is a zero-sum NG by the definition of zero-sum NMG,
Proposition

6
implies that the policy
\widehat{\pi}_{h}(s)
is an
n\epsilon
-approximate NE of the auxiliary-game with the same payoff matrix, and the following inequality is valid for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
:

\displaystyle V_{h,i}^{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle=r_{h,i}(s,\pi)+\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})\pi{{}_{h}}(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq r_{h,i}(s,\widehat{\pi})+\sumop\displaylimits_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{h}(s^{\prime}{\,|\,}s,\bm{a})\widehat{\pi}{{}_{h}}(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s)V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})+n\epsilon.

(27)

Applying the inequality

\displaystyle V_{h+1,i}^{\pi}(s^{\prime})\leq r_{h+1,i}(s^{\prime},\widehat{\pi})+\sumop\displaylimits_{\widetilde{s}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sumop\displaylimits_{\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbb{P}_{h+1}(\widetilde{s}{\,|\,}s^{\prime},\bm{a})\widehat{\pi}{{}_{h+1}}(\bm{a}{\,|\,}s^{\prime})V_{h+2,i}^{\pi}(\widetilde{s})+n\epsilon

into (
27
) continually, iterating this procedure from
h+1
to
H
, yields that for every
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
:

\displaystyle V_{h,i}^{\pi}(s)

\displaystyle\leq V_{h,i}^{\widehat{\pi}}(s)+hn\epsilon.

Moreover, we have that for any
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})^{|{\mathcal{S}}|\times H}

\displaystyle V^{\widehat{\pi}}_{h,i}(s)\geq V^{\pi}_{h,i}(s)-n\epsilon H

\displaystyle\geq V^{\mu,\pi_{-i}}_{h,i}(s)-(n+1)\epsilon H=V^{\mu,\widehat{\pi}_{-i}}_{h,i}(s)-(n+1)\epsilon h,

with a similar observation on the visitation measure under
\pi
and
\widehat{\pi}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
Omitted Details in
Section

4

See
1

Proof.

We separate the proof for the two cases as follows.

Case 1.
\mathcal{E}_{Q}
contains a triangle subgraph.

We will show that for
any
general-sum two-player
turn-based
MG
(A)
, the problem of computing its Markov stationary CCE, which is inherently a
PPAD
-hard problem
[
30
]
, can be reduced to computing the Markov stationary CCE of a three-player zero-sum MG with a triangle structure networked separable interactions
(B)
. Consider an MG
(A)
with two players, players 1 and 2, and a reward function
r_{1}(s,a_{1},a_{2})
and
r_{2}(s,a_{2},a_{1})
, where
a_{i}
is the action of the
i
-th player and
r_{i}
is the reward function of the
i
-th player. The transition dynamics is given by
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a_{1},a_{2})
. In even rounds, player 2’s action space is limited to Noop2, and in odd rounds, player 1’s action space is limited to Noop1, where Noop is an abbreviation of “no-operation”, i.e., the player does not affect the transition dynamics nor reward functions in that round. We denote player 1’s action space in even rounds as
\mathcal{A}_{1,\text{even}}
and player 2’s action space in odd rounds as
\mathcal{A}_{2,\text{odd}}
.

Now, we construct a three-player zero-sum NMG. We set the reward function as
\widetilde{r}_{i}(s,\bm{a})=\sumop\displaylimits_{j\neq i}\widetilde{r}_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})
and
\widetilde{r}_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=-\widetilde{r}_{j,i}(s,a_{j},a_{i})
, where the reward functions are designed so that
\widetilde{r}_{i,j}=-\widetilde{r}_{j,i}
for all
i,j
,
\widetilde{r}_{1,2}+\widetilde{r}_{1,3}=r_{1}
, and
\widetilde{r}_{2,1}+\widetilde{r}_{2,3}=r_{2}
, by introducing a dummy player, player 3. Here
r_{1},r_{2}
are the reward functions in game
(A)
. In even rounds, player 2’s action space is limited to Noop2, and in odd rounds, player 1’s action space is limited to Noop1. Player 3’s action space is always limited to Noop3 in all rounds. The transition dynamics is defined as
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})=\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a_{1},a_{2})
, since
a_{3}
is always Noop3. In other words, player 3’s action does not affect the rewards of the other two players, nor the transition dynamics, and players 1 and 2 will receive the reward as in the two-player turn-based MG. Also, note that due to the turn-based structure of the game
(A)
, the transition dynamics satisfy the decomposable condition in our
Proposition

1
, and it is thus a zero-sum NMG. In fact, every turn-based dynamics can be represented as an ensemble of single-controller dynamics, as we have discussed in
Section

3.1
.
We set the reward function values as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,3}(s,a_{1},\textsf{Noop3})=-\widetilde{r}_{3,1}(s,\textsf{Noop3},a_{1})=r_{1}(s,a_{1},\textsf{Noop2})+r_{2}(s,\textsf{Noop2},a_{1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,3}(s,\textsf{Noop1},\textsf{Noop3})=-\widetilde{r}_{3,1}(s,\textsf{Noop3},\textsf{Noop1})=0

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{2,3}(s,a_{2},\textsf{Noop3})=-\widetilde{r}_{3,2}(s,\textsf{Noop3},a_{2})=r_{1}(s,\textsf{Noop1},a_{2})+r_{2}(s,a_{2},\textsf{Noop1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{2,3}(s,\textsf{Noop2},\textsf{Noop3})=-\widetilde{r}_{3,2}(s,\textsf{Noop3},\textsf{Noop2})=0

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,2}(s,a_{1},\textsf{Noop2})=-\widetilde{r}_{2,1}(s,\textsf{Noop2},a_{1})=-r_{2}(s,\textsf{Noop2},a_{1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,2}(s,\textsf{Noop1},a_{2})=-\widetilde{r}_{2,1}(s,a_{2},\textsf{Noop1})=r_{1}(s,\textsf{Noop1},a_{2}).

Note that the new game
(B)
is still a turn-based game, and thus the Markov stationary CCE is the same as the Markov stationary NE. Also, note that by construction, we know that the equilibrium policies of players
1
and
2
at the Markov stationary CCE of the game
(B)
constitute a Markov stationary CCE of the game
(A)
. If the underlying network is more general and contains a triangle subgraph, we can specify the reward and transition dynamics of these three players as above, and specify all other players to be dummy players, whose reward functions are all zero, and do not affect the reward functions of these three players, nor the transition dynamics.

Case 2.
\mathcal{E}_{Q}
contains a 3-path subgraph.

We will show that for
any
general-sum two-player
turn-based
MG
(A)
, the problem of computing its Markov stationary CCE can also be reduced to computing the Markov stationary CCE of a four-player zero-sum MG with 3-path networked separable interactions
(B)
. Consider an MG
(A)
with two players, players 1 and 2, and a reward function
r_{1}(s,a_{1},a_{2})
and
r_{2}(s,a_{2},a_{1})
, where
a_{i}
is the action of the
i
-th player and
r_{i}
is the reward function of the
i
-th player. The transition dynamics is given by
\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a_{1},a_{2})
. In even rounds, player 2’s action space is limited to Noop2, and in odd rounds, player 1’s action space is limited to Noop1, where Noop is an abbreviation of “no-operation”, i.e., the player does not affect the transition dynamics nor the reward functions in that round. We denote player 1’s action space in even rounds as
\mathcal{A}_{1,\text{even}}
and player 2’s action space in odd rounds as
\mathcal{A}_{2,\text{odd}}
.

Now, we construct a four-player zero-sum NMG with a 3-path network structure. We set the reward function as
\widetilde{r}_{1}(s,\bm{a})=\widetilde{r}_{1,2}(s,a_{1},a_{2})+\widetilde{r}_{1,3}(s,a_{1},a_{3})
and
\widetilde{r}_{2}(s,\bm{a})=\widetilde{r}_{2,1}(s,a_{2},a_{1})+\widetilde{r}_{2,4}(s,a_{2},a_{4})
and
\widetilde{r}_{i,j}(s,a_{i},a_{j})=-\widetilde{r}_{j,i}(s,a_{j},a_{i})
. The reward functions are designed so that

\widetilde{r}_{1,2}+\widetilde{r}_{1,3}=r_{1}
, and
\widetilde{r}_{2,1}+\widetilde{r}_{2,4}=r_{2}
, where
r_{1},r_{2}
are the reward functions in game
(A)
, by introducing dummy players, player 3 and player 4. In even rounds, player 2’s action space is limited to Noop2, and in odd rounds, player 1’s action space is limited to Noop1. Player 3’s action space is always limited to Noop3 in all rounds. Player 4’s action space is always limited to Noop4 in all rounds. The transition dynamics is defined as
\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4})=\mathbb{P}(s^{\prime}\,|\,s,a_{1},a_{2})
, since
a_{3}
is always Noop3 and
a_{4}
is always Noop4. In other words, player 3 and player 4’s actions do not affect the rewards of the other two players, nor the transition dynamics, and players 1 and 2 will receive the reward as in the two-player turn-based MG. Also, note that due to the turn-based structure of the game
(A)
, the transition dynamics satisfy the decomposable condition in our
Proposition

1
, and it is thus a zero-sum NMG. In fact, every turn-based dynamics can be represented as an ensemble of single-controller dynamics, as we have discussed in
Section

3.1
.
We set the reward function values as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,3}(s,a_{1},\textsf{Noop3})=-\widetilde{r}_{3,1}(s,\textsf{Noop3},a_{1})=r_{1}(s,a_{1},\textsf{Noop2})+r_{2}(s,\textsf{Noop2},a_{1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,3}(s,\textsf{Noop1},\textsf{Noop3})=-\widetilde{r}_{3,1}(s,\textsf{Noop3},\textsf{Noop1})=0

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{2,4}(s,a_{2},\textsf{Noop4})=-\widetilde{r}_{4,2}(s,\textsf{Noop4},a_{2})=r_{1}(s,\textsf{Noop1},a_{2})+r_{2}(s,a_{2},\textsf{Noop1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{2,4}(s,\textsf{Noop2},\textsf{Noop4})=-\widetilde{r}_{4,2}(s,\textsf{Noop4},\textsf{Noop2})=0

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,2}(s,a_{1},\textsf{Noop2})=-\widetilde{r}_{2,1}(s,\textsf{Noop2},a_{1})=-r_{2}(s,\textsf{Noop2},a_{1})

\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{1,2}(s,\textsf{Noop1},a_{2})=-\widetilde{r}_{2,1}(s,a_{2},\textsf{Noop1})=r_{1}(s,\textsf{Noop1},a_{2}).

Note that the new game
(B)
is still a turn-based game, and thus the Markov stationary CCE is the same as the Markov stationary NE. Also, note that by construction, we know that the equilibrium policies of players
1
and
2
at the Markov stationary CCE of the game
(B)
constitute a Markov stationary CCE of the game
(A)
. If the underlying network is more general and contains a 3-path subgraph, we can specify the reward and transition dynamics of these four players in the subgraph as above, and specify all other players to be dummy players, whose reward functions are all zero, and do not affect the reward functions of these three players, nor the transition dynamics. This completes the proof.
∎

Proposition 7
.

A connected graph that does not contain a subgraph of a triangle or a 3-path must be a star-shaped graph.

Proof.

If the diameter of a connected graph is exactly 1, then there are only two nodes, which form a star-shaped network. If the diameter of a connected graph is greater than 2, it contradicts the non-existence of a 3-path subgraph. If the diameter of a connected graph is exactly 2,
we denote the middle node as
c
, and the leftmost and rightmost nodes as
l
and
r
. If either
l
or
r
has another neighbor other than
c
, it implies the existence of a 3-path subgraph, which contradicts the assumption. Therefore, the additional nodes other than
l,c,r
, if exist, have to be connected to
c
.
If two neighbors of
c
are directly connected, then
it contradicts the non-existence of a triangle subgraph. Hence, all nodes except
c
have to be connected to
c
while not being connected to each other, which leads to a star-shaped graph.
∎

Appendix D
Omitted Details in
Section

5

We refer to
Appendix

E
for the existing relevant result regarding stochastic approximation. The proof structure for
Appendix

D
follows three steps: (1) find the continuous-time dynamics of the fictitious-play learning dynamics, (2) identify a Lyapunov function for the continuous-time version of the fictitious play (
V(\pi)
or
L(\pi)
), and (3) since the discrete version can be viewed as a perturbed version of the continuous-time dynamics (
Theorem

6
), the limit point of fictitious play is contained in the level set of a Lyapunov function (
Theorem

7
).
Theorem

6
and
Theorem

7
are stated in
Appendix

E
, and these theorems are restatements of
[
52
]
. In this section, with a slight abuse of notation, we interchangeably use
a_{i}
to refer to either an action in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, or a pure strategy
\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, where
\pi_{i}(a_{i})=1
and
\pi_{i}(a^{\prime}_{i})=0
for all
a^{\prime}_{i}\neq a_{i}
.

D.1
Matrix game case

D.1.1
Fictitious-play in zero-sum NGs

We first introduce the fictitious-play dynamics for zero-sum NGs with
\mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E})
, i.e., zero-sum polymatrix games
[
23
,
24
]
, the very same one as in
[
5
,
6
]
: at iteration,
k
, each player
i
maintains a belief of the opponents’ policies,
(\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})
;
she then takes action by best responding to the belief, and then updates the belief as:

\displaystyle\text{Take~action:~~}a_{i}^{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}~~r_{i}(e_{a_{i}},\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)}),\quad~~\text{Update belief:~~}\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k+1)}=\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)}+\alpha^{(k)}(e_{a_{-i}^{(k)}}-\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})

where
r_{i}(\pi)
is the expected payoff under joint policy
\pi
(see
Equation

1
), and
\alpha^{(k)}\geq 0
is the stepsize.
The overall procedure is summarized in
Algorithm

4
.

Algorithm 4

Fictitious Play in zero-sum NGs (
i
-th player)

Choose
\widehat{\pi}_{j}^{(0)}
as a uniform distribution for all
j\in\mathcal{N}/\{i\}

for
each timestep
k=0,1,\dots

do

Take action
a_{i}^{(k)}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}r_{i}(e_{a_{i}},\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})

Observe other players’ action
a_{-i}^{(k)}

Update the policy belief as
\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k+1)}=\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)}+\alpha^{(k)}(e_{a_{-i}^{(k)}}-\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})

end

for

We provide the convergence guarantee of the FP dynamics as follows, showing that zero-sum NGs, i.e., zero-sum polymatrix games
[
23
,
24
]
, possess the fictitious-play property
[
8
]
.

Theorem 4
.

Assuming that
\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha^{(k)}\to\infty
and
\alpha^{(k)}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, then the limit points of
(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})_{k\geq 0}
are the NE of the zero-sum NG.

Proof of
Theorem

4
.

To prove the fictitious-play property, we consider a continuous version of
Algorithm

4
. Assuming that
\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha^{(k)}\to\infty
and
\alpha^{(k)}\to 0
,
[
52
, Proposition 3.27]
states that we can characterize the limit set of
(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})_{k\geq 0}
by considering the following dynamics:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}+\frac{d\pi_{i}}{dt}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}~~r_{i}(e_{a_{i}},\pi_{-i}).

(28)

We define a Lyapunov function as

\displaystyle V(\pi)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\max_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}~~r_{i}(e_{a_{i}},{\pi}_{-i})-r_{i}(\pi)\right).

(29)

Claim 5
.

V(\pi(t))
is a Lyapunov function for (
28
).

Proof.

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}r_{i}(e_{a_{i}},\pi_{-i})
in the formula be
{a_{i}}^{\star}
, then we have

\displaystyle\frac{dV(\pi(t))}{dt}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}e_{a_{i}^{\star}}^{\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}^{\prime}\right)=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}e_{a_{i}^{\star}}^{\intercal}r_{i,j}(e_{a_{j}^{\star}}-\pi_{j})\right)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}-e_{a_{i}^{\star}}^{\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}\right)=-V(\pi(t))

where we use
\pi_{j}^{\prime}
to denote
\frac{d\pi_{j}}{dt}
, and the first equality is derived from the envelope theorem. Since
\max_{a_{i}}r_{i}(a_{i},{\pi}_{-i})\geq r_{i}(\pi)
,
V
is guaranteed to be non-negative. We can thus express
V(t)=V(0)e^{-t}
, indicating that it is decreasing with a linear rate in continuous time.
∎

Consequently,
[
52
, Proposition 3.27]
implies

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\max_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}~~r_{i}(e_{a_{i}},\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})-r_{i}(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})\right)\right)=0

which concludes that every limit point of
(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})_{k\geq 0}
is an NE.
∎

Note that the fictitious-play learning dynamics for zero-sum polymatrix games have also been proposed and analyzed in
[
12
]
, and our result above is a reproduction of it.

D.1.2
Smooth fictitious play in zero-sum NGs

We can also provide guarantees for the learning dynamics of
smooth
fictitious play (may also be referred to as
stochastic
fictitious play later)
[
7
]
, with convergence to the quantal response equilibrium (QRE) of the game
[
114
,
115
]
.

Definition 6
.

A policy
\pi_{\tau}^{\star}=\left(\pi_{\tau,1}^{\star},\cdots,\pi_{\tau,n}^{\star}\right)
is a quantal response equilibrium of the game with regularization coefficient
{\tau}
if the following condition holds

\pi_{\tau,i}^{\star}(a_{i})=\frac{\exp\left(\left[\bm{r}_{i}\pi_{\tau}^{\star}\right]_{a_{i}}/\tau\right)}{\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\exp\left(\left[\bm{r}_{i}\pi_{\tau}^{\star}\right]_{a_{i}^{\prime}}/\tau\right)}

for all
i\in\mathcal{N}
and
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}

[
114
]
.

A QRE always exists in finite games. Moreover, a QRE has an equivalent notion as finding the Nash equilibrium of the game with entropy-regularized payoffs: i.e.,
\pi_{\tau}^{\star}
satisfies that

r_{\tau,i}\left(\pi_{i}^{\prime},\pi_{\tau,-i}^{\star}\right)\leq r_{\tau,i}\left(\pi_{\tau}^{\star}\right),

where

\displaystyle r_{\tau,i}(\pi):=r_{i}(\pi)+\tau\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i})-\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{r,i}}\frac{\tau}{|\mathcal{E}_{r,j}|}\mathcal{H}(\pi_{j})

(30)

and
\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i}):=-\sumop\displaylimits_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\pi_{i}(a_{i})\log(\pi_{i}(a_{i}))
is the Shannon entropy function

[
116
]
. Reference
[
94
]
provided a novel analysis showing that a unique NE exists for zero-sum NGs with entropy regularization (thus the QRE for the unregularized zero-sum NG).

Remark 8
.

In most existing literature
[
53
,
94
]
, the entropy regularized reward is defined as
r_{i}(\pi)+\tau\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i})
. Indeed, note that

\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\left(r_{i}(\pi)+\tau\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i})\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\left(r_{i}(\pi)+\tau\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i})-\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{r,i}}\frac{\tau}{|\mathcal{E}_{r,j}|}\mathcal{H}(\pi_{j})\right)

for any
i\in\mathcal{N}
, so it does not affect the equilibria. Moreover, by defining
r_{\tau,i}(\pi):=r_{i}(\pi)+\tau\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i})-\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{r,i}}\frac{\tau}{|\mathcal{E}_{r,j}|}\mathcal{H}(\pi_{j})
, we can have that
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}r_{\tau,i}(\pi)=0
holds for any joint product policy
\pi
.

Algorithm 5

Stochastic fictitious play in zero-sum NGs (
i
-th player)

Choose
\widehat{\pi}_{j}^{(0)}
as a uniform distribution for all
j\in\mathcal{N}/\{i\}

for
each timestep
k=0,1,\dots

do

Take action
a_{i}^{(k)}\sim\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}r_{\tau,i}(\mu_{i},\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})

Observe other players’ action
a_{-i}^{(k)}

Update the policy belief as
\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k+1)}=\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)}+\alpha^{(k)}(e_{a_{-i}^{(k)}}-\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})

end

for

In
Algorithm

5
, players initialize their beliefs for other players
(\widehat{\pi}_{-i})
as a uniform distribution. They sample from the best-response policy with respect to the entropy-regularized reward, given the beliefs of other players’ policies.
Subsequently, each player observes other players’ actions and updates her beliefs.

Theorem 5
.

Assuming that
\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha^{(k)}\to\infty
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\alpha^{(k)}\to 0
,
(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})_{k\geq 0}
converges to a QRE of the zero-sum NG with probability 1.

Proof of
Theorem

5
.

To prove the fictitious-play property, we consider a continuous-time version of the learning dynamics in
Algorithm

5
. Assuming
\sumop\displaylimits_{k=0}^{\infty}\alpha^{(k)}\to\infty
and
\alpha^{(k)}\to 0
,
[
52
, Proposition 3.27]
states that we can characterize the limit set of
(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})_{k\geq 0}
by considering the following dynamics

\displaystyle\pi_{i}+\frac{d\pi_{i}}{dt}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}~~r_{\tau,i}(\mu_{i},\pi_{-i}).

(31)

We define a Lyapunov function as

\displaystyle V_{\tau}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}~~r_{\tau,i}(\mu_{i},{\pi}_{-i})-r_{\tau,i}(\pi)\right).

Claim 6
.

V_{\tau}(\pi(t))
is a Lyapunov function for (
31
).

Proof.

Let the maximizer of
r_{\tau,i}(\mu_{i},\pi_{-i})
in the formula be
\mu_{i}^{\star}
, which we know is unique due to the regularization. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\frac{dV_{\tau}(\pi(t))}{dt}

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}\mu_{i}^{\star\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}^{\prime}\right)-\left(\tau(\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i}))^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}\mu_{i}^{\star\intercal}r_{i,j}(\mu_{j}^{\star}-\pi_{j})\right)-\left(\tau(\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i}))^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}-\mu_{i}^{\star\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}\right)+\tau(1+\log\pi_{i})^{\intercal}\pi_{i}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}-\mu_{i}^{\star\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}\right)+\tau(1+\log\pi_{i})^{\intercal}(\mu^{\star}_{i}-\pi_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}-\mu_{i}^{\star\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}\right)+\tau(\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i}))+\tau(\log\pi_{i})^{\intercal}(\mu^{\star}_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{j\in\mathcal{E}_{i}}-\mu_{i}^{\star\intercal}r_{i,j}\pi_{j}\right)+\tau(\mathcal{H}(\pi_{i}))-\tau(\mathcal{H}(\mu_{i}))\right)=-V_{\tau}(\pi(t))

where the first equality is derived from the envelope theorem and the last inequality is from Gibbs’ inequality.
Since
\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}r_{\tau,i}(\mu_{i},{\pi}_{-i})\geq r_{\tau,i}(\pi)
,
V
is guaranteed to be non-negative. Therefore, we have
0\leq V(t)\leq V(0)e^{-t}
, indicating that it is decreasing.
∎

Consequently,
[
52
, Proposition 3.27]
implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\left(\max_{\mu_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}~~r_{\tau,i}(\mu_{i},\widehat{\pi}_{-i}^{(k)})-r_{\tau,i}(\widehat{\pi}^{(k)})\right)\right)=0

which concludes that every limit point is a QRE. Since the QRE is unique for zero-sum NGs, we conclude that
(\pi^{(k)})_{k\geq 0}
converges to the QRE of the zero-sum NG.
∎

Remark.

Algorithm

4
converges to an NE, and
Algorithm

5
converges to a QRE. Since the QRE is unique in zero-sum NG for a fixed
\tau
, we can identify the converging point in
Algorithm

5
, while we cannot determine which NE is the converging point in
Algorithm

4
.

D.2
Fictitious-play property of infinite-horizon zero-sum NMGs of a star-shape

Before presenting the results,
we examine some properties of a star-shaped zero-sum NG (i.e., the polymatrix case). We define player 1 as the center player without loss of generality.
First, we can view a star-shaped zero-sum NG as a constant-sum separable star-shaped game, as detailed below.

Proposition 8
.

There exist some
\{c_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}
with
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that a star-shaped zero-sum NG satisfies the following identities:

\displaystyle r_{i,1}^{\intercal}+r_{1,i}=c_{i}\boldsymbol{1}\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}\qquad\text{for every }i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\},\qquad\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}c_{i}=0.

Proof.

By the definition of a zero-sum NG, for arbitrary
\pi_{1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{1}),\{\pi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\in\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\left(\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{1,i}+\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{i,1}^{\intercal}\right)\pi_{i}=0

(32)

which implies
\pi_{1}^{\intercal}(r_{1,i}+r_{i,1}^{\intercal})=c_{i}\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}
for some constant
c_{i}
, since

Equation

32
holds for any
\pi_{i}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
. To be specific,
\pi_{1}^{\intercal}(r_{1,i}+r_{i,1}^{\intercal})\pi_{i}

should be the same when we plugging
\pi_{i}=e_{a_{i}}
for any
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, so that every element of
\pi_{1}^{\intercal}(r_{1,i}+r_{i,1}^{\intercal})
is the same, i.e., there exists some
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi_{1}^{\intercal}(r_{1,i}+r_{i,1}^{\intercal})=c_{i}\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}
. Plugging to
Equation

32
, we have
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}c_{i}=0
. Moreover, this again implies
r_{1,i}+r_{i,1}^{\intercal}=c_{i}\boldsymbol{1}\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}
, since
\pi_{1}^{\intercal}(r_{1,i}+r_{i,1}^{\intercal})=c_{i}\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}
always holds for any
\pi_{1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{1})
by a similar argument as above.
∎

Second, we define the Nash equilibrium value for the center player in a star-shaped zero-sum NG, which is different from the general zero-sum NG case, where there may not exist a unique Nash value
[
24
]
.

Proposition 9
.

There exists a unique Nash equilibrium value for the center player
1
in a star-shaped zero-sum NG (i.e.,
r_{i,j}=0
if
i\neq 1
and
j\neq 1
).

Proof.

Player 1 aims to maximize
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{1,i}\pi_{i}
while player
i\neq 1
aims to maximize
\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{i,1}^{\intercal}\pi_{i}=\pi_{1}^{\intercal}(c_{i}\boldsymbol{1}\boldsymbol{1}^{\intercal}-r_{1,i})\pi_{i}=c_{i}-\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{1,i}\pi_{i}
, with
c_{i}
given in
Proposition

8
. We can solve these problems simultaneously by the following maxmin problem:

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{maximize}}_{\pi_{1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{1})}\mathop{\mathrm{minimize}}_{(\pi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\in\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{1,i}\pi_{i}.

Since
\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{1})
and
\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})
are compact and convex sets, we can use the minimax theorem to show that
\mathop{\mathrm{maximize}}_{\pi_{1}\in\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{1})}\mathop{\mathrm{minimize}}_{(\pi_{i})_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\in\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\pi_{1}^{\intercal}r_{1,i}\pi_{i}
is unique.
∎

Note that there can be multiple Nash equilibrium values for each non-center player, but the
sum
of Nash equilibrium values for non-center players is always unique (see the proof of
Proposition

9
).

Now, we start to prove that fictitious play dynamics given in
Algorithm

1
converges to a Markov stationary NE in infinite-horizon zero-sum NMGs with a star-shaped network.

See
2

Proof.

To prove the result, we consider a continuous-time version of
Algorithm

1
. Using standard two-timescale stochastic approximation techniques
[
52
, Proposition 3.27]
, we can show that the limit set of our FP dynamics can be captured by that of a continuous-time differential inclusion. Before, we define several notation:

\displaystyle\bm{Q}_{i}(s):=(Q_{i,1}(s),\dots,Q_{i,i-1}(s),\bm{0},Q_{i,i+1}(s)\dots,Q_{i,n}(s))\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}_{i}|\times\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}|\mathcal{A}_{i}|}

\displaystyle\bm{Q}(s):=((\bm{Q}_{1}(s))^{\intercal},(\bm{Q}_{2}(s))^{\intercal},\dots,(\bm{Q}_{n}(s))^{\intercal})^{\intercal}\in\mathbb{R}^{\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}|\mathcal{A}_{i}|\times\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}|\mathcal{A}_{i}|}

\displaystyle h(\bm{Q}(s)):=\max_{\mu\in\prodop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}}\Delta(\mathcal{A}_{i})}\left|\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\mu_{1}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)\mu_{i}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\mu_{i}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)\mu_{1}\right)\right|.

(33)

Then, we consider the following differential inclusion for each
s\in{\mathcal{S}}
:

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(s)+\frac{d\pi_{1}(s)}{dt}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)\pi_{i}(s)\right),\,

\displaystyle\pi_{i}(s)+\frac{d\pi_{i}(s)}{dt}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\left(e_{a_{i}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)\pi_{1}(s)\right),\qquad\frac{dQ_{1,i}(s)}{dt}=\frac{dQ_{i,1}(s)}{dt}=\bm{0},

(34)

with a Lyapunov function candidate being

\displaystyle L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)

\displaystyle=\Biggl(\max_{a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)\pi_{i}(s)\right)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\max_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\left(e_{a_{i}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)\pi_{1}(s)\right)-\lambda h(\bm{Q}(s))\Biggr)_{+}

where
h
is defined before,
and
\lambda
is chosen as
1<\lambda<1/\gamma
. Then,
7
below proves that
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)
is a Lyapunov function for (
34
).

Claim 7
.

For every
1<\lambda<1/\gamma
,
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)
is a Lyapunov function of (
34
) for the set
\Lambda=\{(\pi,\bm{Q}):L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)=0\}
.

Proof.

First, we define
V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)
as below:

\displaystyle V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)

\displaystyle=\max_{a_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}}\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)\pi_{i}(s)\right)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\max_{a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}}\left(e_{a_{i}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)\pi_{1}(s)\right)-\lambda h(\bm{Q}(s)).

Then, we have
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)=(V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s))_{+}
. Moreover, let the maximizer of
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)\pi_{i}(s)
be
a_{1}^{\star}
and let the maximizer of
e_{a_{i}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)\pi_{1}(s)
as
a_{i}^{\star}
for
i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{dV_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)}{dt}

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}^{\star}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)\pi_{i}(s)^{\prime}\right)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\left(e_{a_{i}^{\star}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)\pi_{1}^{\prime}(s)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}^{\star}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)(e_{a_{i}^{\star}}-\pi_{i}(s))\right)+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}\left(e_{a_{i}^{\star}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)(e_{a_{1}^{\star}}-\pi_{1}(s))\right)

\displaystyle<-V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)

since
\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{1}^{\star}}^{\intercal}Q_{1,i}(s)e_{a_{i}^{\star}}+\sumop\displaylimits_{i\in\mathcal{N}/\{1\}}e_{a_{i}^{\star}}^{\intercal}Q_{i,1}(s)e_{a_{1}^{\star}}\leq h(\bm{Q}(s))<\lambda h(\bm{Q}(s))
holds by the definition of
h(\bm{Q}(s))
. Therefore,
V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)
is strictly decreasing with respect to time when
V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s)\geq 0
. To emphasize the time dependence of
V_{\lambda}
and
L_{\lambda}
, we will write
V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)
and
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)
.

If
V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)\geq 0
, then
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)=V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)
is strictly decreasing if
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)>0
. Therefore, we can see that if
L_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)>0
so that
V_{\lambda}(\pi,\bm{Q},s,t)>0
, then
